Bigraded Poincaré polynomials and the equivariant cohomology of Rep( C 2 C_{2} )-complexes
Eric Hogle

Abstract
We are interested in computing the Bredon cohomology with coefficients in the constant Mackey functor 𝔽 2 ¯ \underline{\mathbb{F}_{2}} for equivariant Rep ​ ( C 2 ) \text{Rep}(C_{2}) spaces, in particular for Grassmannian manifolds of the form Gr k ⁡ ( V ) \operatorname{Gr}_{k}(V) where V V is some real representation of C 2 C_{2} . It is possible to create multiple distinct Rep ​ ( C 2 ) \text{Rep}(C_{2}) constructions of (and hence multiple filtration spectral sequences for) a given Grassmannian. For sufficiently small examples one may exhaustively compute all possible outcomes of each spectral sequence and determine if there exists a unique common answer. However, the complexity of such a computation quickly balloons in time and memory requirements. We introduce a statistic on 𝕄 2 \mathbb{M}_{2} -modules valued in the polynomial ring OPEN x , y ] x,y] which makes cohomology computation of Rep( C 2 C_{2} )-complexes more tractable, and we present some new results for Grassmannians.



1
Introduction

The theory of
C_{2}
-equivariant spaces and spectra has been a growing area of research especially since the resolution of the Kervaire invariant one problem in
[
HHR
]
. Computations of the Bredon cohomology of these objects using the constant Mackey functor
\underline{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
are nontrivial, but inroads have been made, as by
[
dL
]
,
[
CH1
]
and
[
Clover
]
. This theory continues to prove fruitful, as in
[
BW
]
,
[
dFO
]
and
[
SP
]
.

In particular, work on equivariant vector bundles and Grassmannians has been done as by
[
Dan
]
,
[
NG
]
,
[
CH2
]
and
[
K
]
but much work remains. While the cohomology of infinite-dimensional Grassmannians is solved in
[
Dan
]
and the author of this paper worked out a two families of finite-dimensional spaces in
[
Me
]
, in general the Bredon cohomology of finite-dimensional Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}_{k}({p,q})
is unknown.

Equivariant filtration spectral sequences can be built for these spaces using a Rep(
C_{2}
)-complex construction (see Example
3.16
in this paper and Example 4.1 in
[
Dan
]
), but actually running the sequences is quite difficult. Prior to the results below, given an
E_{1}
page and a candidate answer, even determining whether such an answer was
possible
required an exhaustive investigation of all combinations of differentials that could be supported and the “Kronholm shifts.” of these hypothetical differentials. This brute-force approach is slow.

We present here an invariant which makes answering the question “could this
E_{1}
converge to that module” fast and easy. With this tool in hand a number of new cohomologies are calculated.

2
Preliminaries

The group
C_{2}
has two real representations, the trivial representation
+
and the sign representation
-
. The
RO(C_{2})
-graded Bredon cohomology for
C_{2}
-spaces is therefore bigraded. We used the motivic grading convention in which
{p,q}
denotes
(+)^{\oplus(p-q)}\oplus(-)^{\oplus q}
, so that the two numbers indicate underlying topological dimension and the number of dimensions with nontrivial action. We may also wish to specify a chosen order to a direct sum, for example writing
{-+-}
to mean
-\oplus+\oplus-
. The one-point compactification of these representations are the spheres
S^{p,q}=\widehat{{p,q}}
.

In this paper we abbreviate singular cohomology with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
coefficients of a space
X
by
H^{\ast}(X)
, and for an equivariant space
X
, write
H^{\ast,\ast}(X):=H^{\ast,\ast}(X;\underline{\mathbb{F}_{2}})
. The cohomologies of equivariant spaces in this theory are modules over the cohomology of a point, shown in Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
\mathbb{M}_{2}:=H^{*,*}(\text{pt})

A
\text{Rep}(C_{2})
-complex generalizes CW complexes using discs about the origin in representations. We denote these
D^{p,q}=D({p,q})
. The cohomology of a
\text{Rep}(C_{2})
-complex can in principle be computed using a filtration spectral sequence whose first page is a free
\mathbb{M}_{2}
module made up of one shifted copy of
\mathbb{M}_{2}
corresponding to each cell. Although subsequent pages of the spectral sequence need not be free, we show in
[
Us
]
that the cohomology of such spaces is always a free
\mathbb{M}_{2}
-module of the form
\bigoplus_{i}\Sigma^{a_{i},b_{i}}\mathbb{M}_{2}
. We do this inductively, showing that if a
\text{Rep}(C_{2})
complex has a new cell attached, the resulting space may have some of its free summands ‘shifted’ up in the sign grading, while the new summand corresponding to the new cell has shifted down. These are called Kronholm shifts after their first appearance in
[
K
]
. See Example
2.1
.

Rather than displaying every copy of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
in each bidegree, we will restrict ourselves to depicting the
\mathbb{M}_{2}
-generators with dots as in Figure
2
below
1
1

1

Ignoring all of
\mathbb{M}_{2}
besides the generator makes things easier to represent, but notice for example that while
E_{1}^{2,0}
appears empty,
\Sigma^{2,2}\theta
lives there, which is why a differential from
\Sigma^{1,0}\mathbb{M}_{2}
is possible.
or with rank tables giving the number of generators in each bidegree, as in Figure
4
.

Example 2.1
.

A simple (and in some sense the fundamental) example is when
{}^{2}_{\text{tw}}=\operatorname{Gr}_{1}({3,1})
is built from a Rep-CW structure by identifying
{3,1}
as
{++-}
. In computing the cohomology using this filtration spectral sequence, we see a Kronholm shift from
E_{1}=\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{1,0}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{2,2}\mathbb{M}_{2}
to the cohomology
H^{\ast,\ast}(^{2}_{\text{tw}})=\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{1,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}
. See Figure
2
. We will sometimes describe
\Sigma^{1,0}\mathbb{M}_{2}
as having “shifted” up to
\Sigma^{1,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}
while
\Sigma^{2,2}\mathbb{M}_{2}
shifted down to
\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}
.

Figure 2:

.

In Sections 2.3 and 2.4 of
[
Me
]
we give a method for creating multiple distinct
\text{Rep}(C_{2})
constructions of (and hence multiple spectral sequences for) Grassmannian manifolds of the form
\operatorname{Gr}_{k}(V)
where
V
is some real representation of
C_{2}
. This is also discussed in Example 4.1 of
[
Dan
]
and a little in Example
3.16
of this paper.

We will denote by
\mathcal{U}
the forgetful functor from equivariant spaces to spaces, and by
\psi
the corresponding map from equivariant cohomology to singular cohomology. Finally, we will use
\mathcal{F}
to denote the fixed-set functor on spaces. Note that
\mathcal{F}(S^{a,b})=S^{a-b}
and if
H^{\ast,\ast}(X)=\bigoplus_{i}\Sigma^{a_{i},b_{i}}\mathbb{M}_{2}
then
H^{\ast}(\mathcal{F}X)=\bigoplus_{i}\Sigma^{a_{i}-b_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

3
Computational tools

Given a non-equivariant space
X
, we have a graded
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-module
H^{\ast}(X;\mathbb{F}_{2})
and we can define a classical Poincaré polynomial in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
coefficients,

P(H^{\ast}X)=\sum_{i}\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}(H^{i}(X);\mathbb{F}_{2})x^{i}.

However since our theory is bigraded, we will define another polynomial statistic
\mathcal{P}
in two variables for free
\mathbb{M}_{2}
-modules:

\mathcal{P}\left(\bigoplus_{i}\Sigma^{a_{i},b_{i}}\mathbb{M}_{2}\right)=\sum_{i}x^{a_{i}}y^{b_{i}}.

The natural transformations
\psi
and
\mathcal{F}
can be reflected on the polynomial side with operators
U:f(x,y)\mapsto f(x,1)
and
F:f(x,y)\mapsto f(x,x^{-1})
as shown in Figure
3
.

H^{\ast}(\mathcal{U}(\,\,))
H^{\ast,\ast}(\,\,)
x]
x,y]
\mathcal{P}
P
\psi
U
H^{\ast}(\mathcal{F}(\,\,))
H^{\ast,\ast}(\,\,)
x]
x,y]
\mathcal{P}
P
\circ\mathcal{F}
F

Figure 3:
U(\mathcal{P}(H^{\ast,\ast}X))=P(\psi(H^{\ast,\ast}X))
and
F(\mathcal{P}(H^{\ast,\ast}X))=P(H^{\ast}\mathcal{F}(X))
.

Definition 3.1
.

If
A
and
B
are
\mathbb{M}_{2}
modules, with
B
obtained from
A
by shifting an
\mathbb{M}_{2}
up to a
\Sigma^{0,s}\mathbb{M}_{2}
and a
\Sigma^{n,n+s}\mathbb{M}_{2}
down to a
\Sigma^{n,n}\mathbb{M}_{2}
as in a Kronholm shift
2
2

2

In other words,
A\oplus\Sigma^{0,s}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{n,n}\mathbb{M}_{2}=B\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{n,n+s}\mathbb{M}_{2}
.
define the
Kronholm polynomial
of the shift to be

\displaystyle K_{n,s}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{P}(B)-\mathcal{P}(A)

\displaystyle=x^{0}y^{s}-x^{0}y^{0}+x^{n}y^{n}-x^{n}y^{n+s}

\displaystyle=(1-x^{n}y^{n})(y^{s}-1).

Example 3.2
.

In Example
2.1
we saw a Kronholm shift from
A=\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Sigma^{1,0}\mathbb{M}_{2}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\Sigma^{2,2}\mathbb{M}_{2}}
to the cohomology
B=\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\Sigma^{1,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}}
. See Figure
2
. This shift has Kronholm polynomial

\mathcal{P}(B)-\mathcal{P}(A)=(1+{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}xy}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{2}y})-(1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}x}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}x^{2}y^{2}})=x(1-xy)(y-1)=xK_{1,1}.

Notice that since the generator that shifts up by
s=1
doesn’t start as
\mathbb{M}_{2}
but as
\Sigma^{1,0}\mathbb{M}_{2}
, and since the generator that shifts down is located
n=1
topological dimensions to its right, the Kronholm polynomial of this shift is
x^{1}y^{0}K_{n,s}=xK_{1,1}
.

Example 3.3
.

When
X=\operatorname{Gr}_{2}({4,2})
is built by identifying
{4,2}\cong{++--}
, we have an
E_{1}
page
E_{1}=\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{1,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\oplus 2}\oplus\Sigma^{3,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{4,4}\mathbb{M}_{2}
converging to
H^{\ast,\ast}(X)=\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{1,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{2,1}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{2,2}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{3,2}\mathbb{M}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{4,2}\mathbb{M}_{2}
. See Figure
4
. We can also assign a Kronholm polynomial to this convergence, namely

(x^{4}y^{2}+x^{3}y^{2}+x^{2}y^{2}+x^{2}y+xy+1)-(x^{4}y^{4}+x^{3}y+2x^{2}y+xy+1)=x^{3}yK_{1,2}+x^{2}yK_{1,1}.

Since any change caused by a sequence of Kronholm shifts will show up in the image of
\mathcal{P}
as a sum of Kronholm polynomials with coefficients from
x,y]
, we wish to study all such combinations, motivating the following definition.

3.4
The Kronholm Ideal

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathbb{K}\subseteq x,y]
be the ideal generated by all Kronholm shifts
K_{n,s}
.

Comment 3.6
.

An advantage of working in
x,y]
rather than dealing directly with short exact or spectral sequences in cohomology is that order no longer matters – we don’t need to worry about what shifted first, or which generators on which pages supported the shift. However we also lose access to certain information, for example, when decomposing a Kronholm polynomial, we may be tempted to try to tell the “story” of which Kronholm shifts occurred, but in general there will be ambiguities, as the following lemmas demonstrate.

Example 3.7
.

Suppose there is a sequence of shifts in which the ‘‘downshifted’’ generator from previous shifts becomes the ‘‘upshifted’’ generator in the next.
3
3

3

Say if we compute cohomology each time we attach a new cell to a complex.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
xK_{1,1}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
x^{2}yK_{1,1}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
x^{3}y^{2}K_{1,1}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

This sequence of shifts has the same outcome as a single shift involving only the first and last generator
4
4

4

Say if we just run one spectral sequence.
:

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
xK_{3,1}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

The algebraic fact shown, that
x(1+xy+x^{2}y^{2})K_{1,1}=xK_{3,1}
, illustrates that knowing the beginning and end of the story may not be enough to determine that sequence of events between. This particular type of ambiguity is captured in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.8
.

\displaystyle K_{n,s}=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(xy)^{i}\right)K_{1,s}
.

Algebraically this is immediate:

\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(xy)^{i}\right)K_{1,s}=\frac{1-(xy)^{n}}{1-xy}(1-xy)(y^{s}-1)=(1-x^{n}y^{n})(y^{s}-1)=K_{n,1}.

Example 3.9
.

Similarly, we can imagine multiple shifts happened in sequence in the same topological dimensions, resulting in the same outcome as one “larger” shift. Figure
5
illustrates
5
5

5

This example doesn’t correspond to a possible scenario coming from equivariant spaces. If this bothers you, multiply everything by
x^{3}
for a scenario where no bidegree’s weight exceeds its topological dimension.
the algebraic fact that
K_{1,3}=(1+y+y^{2})K_{1,1}
. This too can be generalized with a lemma.

Lemma 3.10
.

\displaystyle K_{1,s}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{s-1}y^{j}\right)K_{1,1}
.

In fact, all Kronholm shifts relate back to the fundamental shift
K_{1,1}
.

Theorem 3.11
.

\mathbb{K}
is principally generated:
\mathbb{K}=(K_{1,1})
.

Proof 1.

Note that because Kronholm shifts are invisible to the natural transformations
F
and
U
,
\mathbb{K}\subseteq\ker(F)\cap\ker(U)=(1-xy)\cap(y-1)=(K_{1,1})
and of course
K_{1,1}\in\mathbb{K}
.
∎

Proof 2.

It follows from the lemmas
3.8
and
3.10
that

K_{n,s}=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(xy)^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{s-1}y^{j}\right)K_{1,1}.

∎

We can now justify the following definition.

Definition 3.12
.

For
\mathbb{M}_{2}
-modules
A
and
B
related by Kronholm shifts, the
shift story
from
A
to
B
is the polynomial

f(x,y)=\frac{\mathcal{P}(B)-\mathcal{P}(A)}{K_{1,1}}.

Example 3.13
.

The Kronholm shift in Example
3.2
corresponds to a shift story of
f(x,y)=x=x^{1}y^{0}
, indicating a differential from
\Sigma^{1,0}\mathbb{M}_{2}
causing it to shift up.

But as the ambiguities in Examples
3.7
and
3.9
illustrate, this story is only a story. The shift story for
\operatorname{Gr}_{2}({4,2})
in Example
3.3
is
f(x,y)=x^{2}y+x^{3}y+x^{3}y^{2}
. However
E_{1}
has no generators in degree
(3,2)
. The appearance of the term
x^{3}y^{2}
is an artifact of the identity
x^{3}yK_{1,2}=x^{3}y(1+y)K_{1,1}
from Lemma
3.10
.

A reader interested only in computing Bredon cohomology may safely skip the following section.

3.14
Aside: Another invariant

Comment 3.15
.

We could define a coarser invariant on
\text{Rep}(C_{2})
-complexes
X
given by the image of
\mathcal{P}(H^{\ast,\ast}(X))
under the quotient
x,y]\to x,y]/\mathbb{K}
. This is still not as coarse as the traditional Poincaré polynomial of the underlying space. For
n
-dimensional manifolds we can choose representatives for these equivalence classes to take the form
[p(x)+x^{n}y^{T}]
where
p
has degree
n-1
, and we can speak of the
total weight

T
associated to a space. For instance, looking back at Example
3.2
,

[\mathcal{P}(H^{\ast,\ast}({P}^{2}_{tw}))]=[1+xy+x^{2}y]=[(1+x)+x^{2}y^{2}]

giving total weight
T({P}^{2}_{tw})=2
while from Example
3.3
,

[\mathcal{P}(H^{\ast,\ast}(\operatorname{Gr}_{2}{4,2}))]=[(1+x+2x^{2}+x^{3})+x^{4}y^{8}]

and so
T(\operatorname{Gr}_{2}{4,2})=8
.

This invariant
T
is Kronholm-shift agnostic. For Grassmannians, it can be computed without running a spectral sequence since it can be read off of the first page by simply adding up weights. Playing with some computed examples and the
[
oeis
]
leads to the formula in Theorem
for these spaces’ total weights.

Before stating the theorem, we recall that each Schubert cell can be indexed by a Young diagram. The cell’s dimension corresponds to the number of degrees of freedom in a matrix.

.

\par\par\begin{thm}$$T(\operatorname{Gr}_{k}({p,q}))=(p-q)q\binom{p-2}{k-1}.$$\end{thm}\par\par\@proof\par\par Wewilldeliberatelyovercount,summingdegreesoffreedomwithnontrivialactionacrossall\binom{p}{q}``sprinklings,^{\prime\prime}thatisallofthewaysoforderingqcopiesof-amongp-qcopiesof+tobuild{p,q}.The\binom{p}{k}differentSchubertcellshaveonaverage\frac{k(p-k)}{2}degreesoffreedombysymmetryofYoungdiagramsfittingwithak\times(p-k)box.EachdegreeoffreedomineachSchubertcellwillhaveasignactionwhenhorizontalpositionandthatifitsrow^{\prime}slastnonzeroentrycorrespondtodifferentrepresentations,asdiscussedabove.Theremainingp-2horizontalpositionsmaybeoccupiedbytheremainingq-1signrepresentationin\binom{p-2}{q-1}ways.Andsosummingoverallsprinklingssandnotingthattotalweightisindependentofthischoiceof{p,q},wehave$$\sum_{s}T(s)=\binom{p}{q}T=\underbrace{\binom{p}{k}}_{\text{\# cells}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{k(p-k)}{2}}_{\text{avg cell dim}}\cdot\underbrace{2\binom{p-2}{q-1}}_{\text{\#}{-}\text{ actions}}$$Andthus$$T=\binom{p}{k}k(p-k)\cdot\frac{\binom{p-2}{q-1}}{\binom{p}{q}}=p(p-1)\binom{p-2}{k-2}\cdot\frac{(p-q)q}{p(p-1)}=(p-q)q\binom{p-2}{k-2}.$$\qed\end@proof\par\begin{example}Suppose we want to check $T(\operatorname{Gr}_{1}{3,2})$. There are $\binom{3}{2}$ ways to identify ${3,2}$.\\
\begin{figure}\begin{center}\begin{tabular}[]{r|c|c|c}&${--+}$&${-+-}$&${+--}$\\
\hline\cr\phantom{$\displaystyle\int$}2-cell&\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$-$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$-$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}&\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$+$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$-$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}&\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$-$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$+$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\\
\phantom{$\displaystyle\int$}1-cell&\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$+$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}&\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$-$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}&\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt\hbox to10.93333pt{\hfil$-$\hfil}\vrule height=8.74669pt,width=0.3pt,depth=2.18663pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\\
\phantom{$\displaystyle\int$}0-cell&*&*&*\end{tabular}\end{center}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{6}}{Three ways to construct the same space}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 6}}{Three ways to construct the same space}}}
\end{figure}We count $\binom{3}{2}T=\underbrace{\binom{3}{1}}_{\text{\# cells}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{2}{2}}_{\text{avg dim}}\underbrace{2\binom{1}{1}}_{\#-}=3\cdot 1\cdot 2$. the last term indicates that any given degree of freedom will be signed exactly twice, as can be checked by reading horizontally in Figure \ref{threeways}.\\
\end{example}\par\begin{comment}This combinatorial proof tells a story that still requires some manipulation to produce our simpler formula. It would be nice to learn that the formula itself can be more directly interpreted combinatorially.
\end{comment}\par Thisendstheasideaboutx,y]/\mathbb{K}.Wenextexplainanalgorithmtousetheideal\mathbb{K}toefficientlycomputesomeequivariantspectralsequencesforcohomology.\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Algorithm Design}\par\par AnaiveapproachtocomputingH^{\ast,\ast}\operatorname{Gr}_{k}{p,q}mightlooklike\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Naive algorithm}\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Calculate, for each of the $\binom{p}{q}$ ways of identifying ${p,q}$ as an ordered direct sum of one-dimensional representations, the $E_{1}$ page of the filtration spectral sequence for $\operatorname{Gr}_{k}{p,q}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@For the $i^{\text{th}}$ $E_{1}$ page, find the set $D_{i}$ of possible differentials, based on bidegree. Since each could be zero or nonzero, this gives roughly $2^{|D_{i}|}$ possible $E_{\infty}$ pages, each an associate graded of a free $\mathbb{M}_{2}$ module. Calculate each of these, creating a set $P_{i}$ of free modules to which the $i^{\text{th}}$ spectral sequence could possibly converge. Repeat for $1\leq i\leq\binom{p}{q}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Calculate the common set $\bigcap P_{i}$ of possible answers. If this is a singleton set, we have our cohomology.
\end{itemize}\par Whilepracticableforsmallexamples,thisapproachiscomputationallyexpensive.Instead,weusetheideasoftheprevioussectionforsomerefinements.\par Definethe\textbf{tension}ofafree\mathbb{M}_{2}moduleAbethenumber\mathcal{P}(A)(1,2).SinceK_{1,1}(1,2)=-1,anysequenceofKronholmshiftswillreducetension.Thisgivesaneasilycomputableheuristic.A``relaxed"E_{1}pagewiththelowertensionshouldintuitivelyhavefewerpossibleE_{\infty}pages,ascomparedtoahigher-tensionE_{1}whichhasmorepotentialKronholmshifts.\par Giventwofree\mathbb{M}_{2}modulesAandB,wecaneasilyanswerthequestionofwhetherBisaKronholmshiftofA.First,thepolynomial\mathcal{P}(B)-\mathcal{P}(A)mustliein\mathbb{K}.Butfurtheritmustbethecasethatthe\textbf{shift story}f(x,y)=\frac{\mathcal{P}(B)-\mathcal{P}(A)}{K_{1,1}}isapolynomialwithstrictlypositivecoefficients,sowearedoing(ratherthanundoing)shiftstoAinordertogetB.Thiscanbequicklycheckedwithacomputeralgebrasystem.\par Withthesetools,weexecutethefollowingsteps.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Improved algorithm}\par\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Calculate, for each of the $\binom{p}{q}$ ways of identifying ${p,q}$ as a direct sum of one-dimensional representations, the $E_{1}$ page of the filtration spectral sequence for $\operatorname{Gr}_{k}{p,q}$. Discard any duplicates.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Find the page with the lowest tension. Call it the $r^{\text{th}}$ $E_{1}$ page. Find the set of $D_{r}$ of possible differentials and compute all of the roughly $2^{|D_{r}|}$ free modules to which this page could possibly converge. Discard duplicates. We now have a set $P_{r}$ of possible answers starting from the most relaxed construction.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@For each $i\neq r$ we have an $E_{1}$ page that must converge to the right answer. Any candidate answer in $P_{r}$ to which some other $E_{1}$ page \emph{cannot} converge is therefore incorrect.\par But first we reduce redundancy. If the shift story indicates that the $i^{\text{th}}$ $E_{1}$ page can shift to the $j^{\text{th}}$ $E_{1}$ page, discard the former, as the latter has a more restrictive set of possible outcomes.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@For each remaining $E_{1}$ page and each possible answer $A\in P_{r}$, use the shift story $\frac{\mathcal{P}(A)-\mathcal{P}(E_{1})}{K_{1,1}}$ to check if $A$ is a Kronholm shift of $E_{1}$. If not, discard $A$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@The list of un-discarded elements of $P_{r}$ is our set of possible answers. With any luck, $|P_{r}|=1$.\\
\end{enumerate}\par\begin{example}\par If we wish to compute $H^{\ast,\ast}\operatorname{Gr}_{3}{6,3}$, the steps look like:
\par\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Of the $\binom{6}{3}=20$ possible constructions, there are 6 unique $E_{1}$ pages.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@One of these has Poincar\'{e} polynomial $p_{r}(x,y)=x^{9}y^{5}+x^{8}y^{4}+2x^{7}y^{4}$
$$+x^{6}y^{4}+x^{5}y^{5}+2x^{6}y^{3}+2x^{5}y^{3}+x^{4}y^{4}+2x^{4}y^{2}+2x^{3}y^{2}+x^{3}y+2x^{2}y+xy+1$$with a tension of $p_{r}(1,2)=201$, the lowest value of the six. After a few dozen milliseconds, we can list the 24 unique free modules associated to all possible $E_{\infty}$ pages for this $E_{1}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Three of the remaining five $E_{1}$ pages can shift to others, leaving only two non-redundant starting points.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@One of these eliminates 11 of our possible answers since they are not valid Kronholm shifts. The next $E_{1}$ page eliminates 7 more.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Unfortunately this still leaves $|P_{r}|=6$. While quite a few new calculations can be successfully executed in this way (see Section \ref{results}) in general still more work is required. In particular, for the completion of this calculation of $H^{\ast,\ast}\operatorname{Gr}_{3}{6,3}$ using some extra tricks, see Section \ref{finishing363}.
\end{enumerate}\par\end{example}\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Results}\par HerewesummarizenewandpreviouslyknownresultsforH^{\ast,\ast}(\operatorname{Gr}_{k}{p,q}).\par Notethatbecause\perp:\operatorname{Gr}_{k}{p,q}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{p-k}{p,q}isanisomorphismand\operatorname{Gr}_{k}{p,q}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{k}{p,p-q},wecanrestricttok,q\leq\frac{p}{2}.AformulaforH^{\ast,\ast}(\operatorname{Gr}_{1}{p,q})=\mathbb{P}({p,q})appearsin\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{K}{}{}]}.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{$q=1$}\par AformulaforH^{\ast,\ast}(\operatorname{Gr}_{k}{p,1}).appearsin\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Me}{}{}]}.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{$q=2$}\par AformulaforH^{\ast,\ast}(\operatorname{Gr}_{2}{p,2}).appearsin\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Me}{}{}]}.ThealgorithmfromSection\ref{section:alg}alsoyieldsH^{\ast,\ast}(\operatorname{Gr}_{3}{6,2}).SeeFigure\ref{fig:362}.\par\par\begin{figure}\begin{center}\hbox to114.5pt{\vbox to59.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\end{figure}\par Considerthecofibersequence\par\par$$\operatorname{Gr}_{3}{6,3}\xhookrightarrow{i}\operatorname{Gr}_{3}(({+-})^{\oplus\infty})\to R:=\frac{\operatorname{Gr}_{3}({+-})^{\oplus\infty}}{\operatorname{Gr}_{3}{6,3}}.$$\par NotethatinsingularcohomologyH_{\text{sing}}^{2}(R)=H_{\text{sing}}^{3}(R)=0andsointhelongexactsequenceofthiscofibersequenceweget$$i^{*}_{\text{sing}}:H_{\text{sing}}^{2}\operatorname{Gr}_{3}(6)\cong H_{\text{sing}}^{2}(\operatorname{Gr}\infty)\cong 2\oplus 2.$$NowsupposeforacontradictionthatH^{2,1}(\operatorname{Gr}_{3}{6,3})=0asincase(vi).Thennecessarily(i^{*})^{2,1}=0andso(i^{*}_{\text{sing}})^{2}=\psi((i^{*})^{2,1})\oplus\psi((i^{*})^{2,2})cannotbefullrank,andwehaveourcontradiction.Andsoonlyoption(iv)remains.SeeFigure\ref{fig:363}.\par\begin{figure}\begin{center}\hbox to142.67pt{\vbox to85.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\end{figure}\par\par\begin{comment}Note that Figure \ref{fig:363} is the only result in this paper that is not fully ``relaxed,'' that is, for which a Kronholm shift might have occurred but didn't. It may be that our algorithm from Section \ref{section:alg} yields conclusive results \emph{only} when every possible shift does in fact occur, and also when some $E_{1}$ page exists which provides evidence of its occurrence. Further calculations like the one in this section might cast some light.
